I drive transit, you know, the city buses, and dispatch has been—well, they've been doing things. Tracking me. And not just the normal GPS tracking, because obviously they have to know where the buses are, I get that, that's just part of the job. But it's the route changes. They keep making these tiny little adjustments to my schedule, and I know they're monitoring how I react to it through the computer system in the cab. And then there's the radio. The dispatcher will make these little comments, just these offhand remarks that sound normal to everyone else, but I know they're directed at me. Like, 'watch your speed on 4th,' when I'm not even on 4th yet, but they know I'm about to turn there. And my supervisor, she tells me, 'Oh, it's just normal oversight, everyone gets audited, it's just standard compliance,' but I'm not stupid. I can tell. I see the way the other drivers look at me in the breakroom. Certain drivers, especially the ones on the early shift, I know they're reporting back on me. They're watching me punch in, watching how long I take to do my pre-trip inspection, and they're feeding it all back to compliance. So, yeah, that's... that's why I'm here. Because it's just getting to be a lot to deal with every single shift.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? Like... like imaginary voices? No, no, definitely not. It's nothing like that. I mean, my hearing is perfectly fine, you know? Actually, it's probably better than fine, because when you're sitting in that driver's seat for eight, sometimes nine hours a day if I pick up an extra shift, you really have to be hyper-aware of every single sound around you. You're listening to the engine hum, the pneumatic doors hissing, the air brakes, the passengers shuffling around in the back... and of course, you're always, always listening to the radio. \n\nBut it's not like I'm sitting there hearing ghosts or, you know, invisible people talking to me in my head. The things I hear are completely real. When dispatch comes over the comms, that is a real person, sitting down at the control center, speaking into a real microphone. It's just... it's what they choose to say, and how they say it, that's the problem. Like I mentioned with the 4th street thing earlier? That was a real voice over the speaker, it was Greg from the afternoon shift, but he was saying things he shouldn't have known to say at that exact moment. And in the breakroom, when the other drivers are huddled up by the coffee maker and talking low, I might not always make out every single word they're whispering, but I hear the tone. I hear the way they suddenly stop talking when I walk over to the vending machine. So, no, I'm not hearing things that aren't there. I'm just hearing exactly what they're doing, and they think I don't notice it, but I absolutely do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your thinking or behavior that other people have said seem disorganized or unusual?\n\nPatient: Well, 'unusual' is such a subjective word, isn't it? I mean, who gets to decide what's usual? But... I suppose, yes, my supervisor and a couple of the other drivers have made some comments lately about how I'm doing things. Like, in the dispatch office before my shift, there's this big magnetic board where we have all the route slips and driver assignments. And recently, I've just had to make sure they're arranged... properly. Not just thrown up there haphazardly, because that's how mistakes happen, that's how the whole system gets thrown off. So I'll spend some time—maybe a little longer than I used to, I admit—moving the magnets around, lining them up in a very specific pattern, making sure the colors coordinate with the shift times and the route numbers. It just has to be right, or else the whole board feels like it's going wrong, like the whole day is going to derail. \n\nAnd then... well, there's this other thing. When people talk to me, especially the other drivers or dispatch, I've found myself needing to repeat the last few words they say before I answer them. Like, if Greg says, 'Bus 42 is ready for you,' I'll say, 'ready for you... yeah, I'll take the keys.' It just sort of slips out. I don't even always realize I'm doing it until I see their faces. They get this look, like I'm being difficult on purpose or trying to mock them, but I'm not! I'm really not. I'm just... I have to process it that way to make sure I've got it exactly right, because if I miss a detail, if I don't repeat it to lock it in, who knows what they'll report back to compliance? But they don't understand that. They just stare at me and whisper to each other when they think I'm out of earshot, calling me weird or saying I'm disorganized, when really I'm the only one trying to keep everything perfectly organized so the whole system doesn't collapse.\n\nDoctor: How much have these experiences affected your ability to do your job?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely having an impact, I won't deny that. I mean, I try to keep everything together, I really do, but with all the tracking and the magnets and making sure I don't miss a single word anyone says... it takes up so much time. And energy. So, yeah, at work, things have been slipping. I'm usually so good about my route reports, you know, filling out every little detail about passenger counts and traffic delays and any mechanical issues, but lately I've just... I've fallen so far behind on them. There's a stack of them in my locker right now, just sitting there, because I can't seem to focus long enough to write it all down without second-guessing what compliance is going to do with that information. \n\nAnd it's not just the paperwork. When I have to relay instructions over the radio, or talk to the other drivers during shift changes, I guess... I guess I've gotten confusing. At least, that's what they say. The other drivers have been complaining to my supervisor, saying they can't follow what I'm telling them, which is incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to be precise! But they get annoyed, and then they report me, and it just makes the whole situation worse. \n\nAnd honestly, it doesn't stop when I clock out. It bleeds into everything. When I get home, I'm just so drained from being on high alert all day, from analyzing every word and every look, that I just... I can't do anything. My husband, bless him, he's had to take over basically everything. He's doing all the cooking now, making all the meals, which he never used to have to do because I always had a meal plan for the week, color-coded and everything on the fridge. But now he's doing that, and he's handling all the phone calls, paying the bills, doing all the basic planning for the house... because I just can't keep up. I sit down on the couch and my mind is just racing with everything from the depot, and I can't even figure out what we're supposed to have for dinner, let alone actually make it. So, yes, it's... it's affecting a lot. It's affecting everything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no, I don't see things. I mean, my vision is 20/20, which you absolutely need when you're driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic during rush hour. You have to see everything—every pedestrian stepping off the curb, every cyclist weaving between lanes, the exact timing of the yellow lights. But I'm not seeing... like, hallucinations or whatever you want to call it. I'm not seeing monsters or angels or things that aren't physically standing right there on the pavement. \n\nEverything I see is completely, one hundred percent real. It's just... I notice the details that other people miss, or maybe they just pretend they don't see them. Like the way the dispatch manager, Sarah, will look at Greg right after I walk into the room, and they do this little nod. It's a real nod, I see it happen with my own two eyes, but it's what the nod means that's the issue. Or the way a passenger will get on the bus, scan their pass, and then look directly into the rearview mirror at me for exactly three seconds before sitting down. They are really doing that. I see them do it. I'm not making it up, I'm not hallucinating the passenger or the mirror or the look. \n\nSo, no, I don't see things that aren't there. I just see exactly what is there, and I see how it all connects, even when everyone else is walking around with blinders on, acting like everything is completely normal when it's obviously not. I see the way the route maps in the breakroom have been slightly shifted, just a fraction of an inch, but enough to change the grid alignment. That's a real physical change, not a vision. So, no, my eyes are perfectly fine. I just see what they're actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone else can put thoughts into your mind or take thoughts out of it?\n\nPatient: Put thoughts... put thoughts into my mind? Or take them out? No, no, no, that’s... that’s not how it works. That sounds like something out of a movie. My thoughts are entirely my own. I mean, if anything, the problem is that I have too many of my own thoughts, you know? \n\nLike I was saying before about the tracking and the route slips and making sure everything is perfectly aligned... my brain is just constantly running, constantly analyzing every single detail, every look, every shift in the schedule. Nobody is putting those thoughts there. I’m the one generating them because I’m the only one actually paying attention to what’s going on around the depot. \n\nAnd as for taking thoughts out? Honestly, I wish! I really, really wish someone could just reach in and scoop out the compliance regulations and the passenger counts and the traffic grids for just, I don't know, five minutes so I could actually relax when I sit on my couch at home. But no, they are all jammed in there, and they stay right there. It’s just me in my head. \n\nI mean, they can watch me, obviously. They can document what I do, they can whisper about me behind my back in the breakroom, but they can’t get inside my actual brain. They have to rely on external things, which is exactly why they do the nodding and the mirror checking that I told you about. If they could just beam thoughts into my head or steal my thoughts, they wouldn’t need to go through all that trouble with watching me, would they? They wouldn't need to be so sneaky about it in the dispatch office. So, no, my mind is entirely my own jurisdiction. It’s a very, very crowded jurisdiction right now, and it's exhausting to keep up with it all, but it's all me. Nobody else is in there.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs and experiences been going on continuously for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Well, continuously... yes, I suppose you could say that. It hasn't really stopped, not for a single day. If I think back on exactly when this all started to really ramp up... it's been about eight months now. Yes, eight months. \n\nIt was right around the time they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. You see, our regular lead, Tom, he had to take an unexpected medical leave—something with his back, or at least that's what they told us, though I have my own thoughts on the timing of that, but anyway. They needed someone to step up and cover the desk, and because I've always been so meticulous with my route reports and my scheduling, they picked me. And at first, I thought, okay, this is a great opportunity. I'm going to be sitting at the central desk, I'll have access to all the master grids, the real-time GPS tracking for the entire fleet, the direct lines to compliance... I thought it would be a good thing.\n\nBut that's when I really started to see it all. When you're just driving your own route, you only see your own little piece of the puzzle, right? You see your passengers, your traffic lights, your specific schedule. But when they put me in that lead role eight months ago, suddenly I was looking at the whole board. I was seeing how all the pieces were moving at once. And that's when I noticed the discrepancies. That's when I saw how the compliance officers would log certain delays but completely ignore others, and how Sarah in management started having these closed-door meetings that weren't on the official calendar. \n\nAnd even after Tom came back and I went back to my regular driving route, I couldn't unsee it. Once you realize how closely everything is being monitored, how the schedules are being manipulated behind the scenes, you can't just turn that awareness off. So yes, for the last eight months, it's been building and building. Every day I go in, I notice another detail, another look, another slight shift in the route maps. It's just been this constant, steady accumulation of evidence since I sat at that dispatch desk. So, definitely more than six months. Eight months of just... having my eyes wide open to it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers can control your actions or move your body from afar?\n\nPatient: Control my actions? Move my body? No, no, I... I'm the one driving the bus, Doctor. Literally and figuratively. Nobody is, like, pulling my strings like a puppet. \n\nI mean, if they could control my actions, they wouldn't need to try so hard to intimidate me, would they? They wouldn't need to do the nodding, or the mirror checking, or the little subtle shifts in the breakroom schedules. If Sarah could just make me walk into her office and hand over my logbook, she would. But she can't. She has to wait for me to submit it at the end of my shift, exactly at 4:15 PM, like I always do. She has to rely on the system, on the rules, because she has absolutely no physical control over me. \n\nMy body is my own. When I get up in the morning, when I put on my uniform—the blue shirt, the navy trousers, the little name tag that never quite sits straight no matter how many times I adjust the pin—I'm doing all of that. My hands, my arms, my legs. It's all me. \n\nAnd honestly, if someone else were controlling my body, I think I'd be a lot less exhausted at the end of the day. But no, it's me. I'm the one doing the work. I'm the one doing all the moving. They just watch what I do. They can't make me do anything.\n\nDoctor: During the times you’ve felt most certain that dispatch and your coworkers were targeting you, have you also had a period of feeling unusually depressed or unusually energized with much less need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Or... unusually energized? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, don't get me wrong, the whole situation is incredibly stressful, like I was telling you about the knot in my shoulders and constantly having to watch the mirrors and monitor the dispatchers. I am up every single morning at 4:30 AM sharp when my alarm goes off. I make my coffee—just black, because I don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when I need to be at the depot by 5:15 to do a proper, thorough pre-trip inspection of my vehicle before they even open the main gates. I check the tire pressure, the fluid levels, the air brakes... I do all of it. So I'm not, you know, moping around or losing interest in my life. I'm just highly focused. Highly, highly focused on the situation at hand and making sure I don't make a single mistake they can use against me. \n\nAnd as for being unusually energized or not needing sleep? Oh, believe me, Doctor, I need sleep. I am so incredibly tired by the time I get home. Like I said before, the physical and mental toll of driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic while simultaneously keeping track of all the surveillance and the coded route slips... it drains you. It completely drains you. I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying up all night scrubbing my kitchen baseboards or whatever people do when they have too much energy. \n\nBy the time 9:00 PM rolls around, I am practically collapsing into my bed. I *want* to sleep. Now, sometimes it takes a while to actually drift off because my brain is still reviewing the day's events, trying to piece together what Sarah meant when she deliberately moved the breakroom chair two inches to the left, but I definitely *need* the rest. I'm not running on two hours of sleep and feeling like a superhero. I'm just... I'm just a normal person trying to do her job safely while everyone at the dispatch office is playing these elaborate games, you know? So no, no extreme moods like that. Just the completely normal exhaustion of dealing with all of this day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the dispatcher or coworkers are inserting or broadcasting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Inserting thoughts? Like... beaming things into my brain? No, doctor, no. It’s nothing like a science fiction movie or anything like that. My thoughts are completely my own, just like my body is my own. Every single time I have to detour, even if it's just one block because of that water main break on 4th Avenue last week, it gets flagged on their end. \n\nAnd then they make these little comments on the two-way radio. Just tiny, subtle things that probably sound completely harmless to anyone else listening in, but I know exactly what they mean. I know the difference. I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me, too. Like Gary, who drives Route 15. They’re tracking my every move through the computers, the radios, and the other drivers acting as informants. It's all happening right out in the open. You just have to be paying close enough attention to see how all the pieces connect.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers are doing anything impossible or supernatural, like using telepathy, reading your mind, or controlling events from a distance?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, Doctor, no, it’s really nothing like that. No magic, no supernatural powers, no telepathy or controlling things with their minds. It’s not some impossible sci-fi movie plot. It’s very real, very grounded technology, which honestly makes it so much worse because there's nowhere to hide from it. \n\nI'm here because dispatch and compliance have been relentlessly tracking my route changes through the computer system. The CAD system is incredibly precise, you see. It updates the GPS coordinates every three seconds. And I know—believe me, I know—what everyone says about it. Just... just these tiny, pointed micro-aggressions broadcast over the open channel. Like, I’ll have to detour around a stalled delivery truck on 8th Street, a completely standard, by-the-book maneuver. It’s always directed at me. \n\nAnd it’s not just the people in the dispatch office sitting behind the computer screens, either. I mentioned Gary earlier with his clipboard, but it’s Brenda, too. Brenda drives the 60-foot articulated bus on Route 9. Tuesday morning, she pulled into the layover zone at the exact same time as me, which isn't even on her scheduled timetable, and she parked at this very specific, deliberate angle so her side mirror was perfectly aligned with my cab. She sat there for ten minutes, just typing on her phone and looking up at me. Typing and looking. Typing and looking. \n\nSo, you see, they don't need to control events from a distance with magic or read my mind. They don't need anything supernatural. They have the computers, they have the radios, and they have each other acting as informants. It’s a completely physical, real-world surveillance operation they're running against me, right out in the open.\n\nDoctor: What would you say is the strongest evidence that dispatch and your coworkers are targeting you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor, I feel like I've been laying out the evidence this whole time. It’s the... it's the sheer, undeniable coordination of it all. \n\nI’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, and it's not just some vague feeling I have, it's literally documented in the CAD logs. It's the timing of it that proves it. \n\nIt’s just so incredibly frustrating because... well, because even when people say it’s just normal oversight, I know for a fact it isn't. People love to say that to me. It is *not* a dispatcher making a hyper-specific, coded comment about adhering to approved thoroughfares the literal second my left tire crosses a lane line to safely bypass a stalled delivery truck. \n\nAnd the absolute clincher, the thing that proves it’s not just some glitch in the system or one overzealous dispatcher with a grudge, is the coworkers. Like I was saying before about Gary and Brenda... you don't just sit at a downtown transit center with a clipboard staring directly at another driver's windshield for four minutes straight for no reason. They are acting as eyes on the ground for dispatch. \n\nSo when you take the computer tracking, the radio comments, and the other drivers acting as informants all working perfectly in sync... I mean, what more evidence do you need? It’s a complete, closed loop of surveillance. That's the strongest evidence. When I get home, I'm just so drained from being on high alert all day, from analyzing every word and every look, that I just... I can't do anything. My husband, bless him, he's had to take over basically everything. He's doing all the cooking now, making all the meals, which he never used to have to do because I always had a meal plan for the week, color-coded and everything on the fridge. But now he's doing that, and he's handling all the phone calls, paying the bills, doing all the basic planning for the house... because I just can't keep up. I sit down on the couch and my mind is just racing with everything from the depot, and I can't even figure out what we're supposed to have for dinner, let alone actually make it. So, yes, it's... it's affecting a lot. It's affecting everything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no, I don't see things. I mean, my vision is 20/20, which you absolutely need when you're driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic during rush hour. You have to see everything—every pedestrian stepping off the curb, every cyclist weaving between lanes, the exact timing of the yellow lights. But I'm not seeing... like, hallucinations or whatever you want to call it. I'm not seeing monsters or angels or things that aren't physically standing right there on the pavement. \n\nEverything I see is completely, one hundred percent real. It's just... I notice the details that other people miss, or maybe they just pretend they don't see them. Like the way the dispatch manager, Sarah, will look at Greg right after I walk into the room, and they do this little nod. It's a real nod, I see it happen with my own two eyes, but it's what the nod means that's the issue. Or the way a passenger will get on the bus, scan their pass, and then look directly into the rearview mirror at me for exactly three seconds before sitting down. They are really doing that. I see them do it. I'm not making it up, I'm not hallucinating the passenger or the mirror or the look. \n\nSo, no, I don't see things that aren't there. I just see exactly what is there, and I see how it all connects, even when everyone else is walking around with blinders on, acting like everything is completely normal when it's obviously not. I see the way the route maps in the breakroom have been slightly shifted, just a fraction of an inch, but enough to change the grid alignment. That's a real physical change, not a vision. So, no, my eyes are perfectly fine. I just see what they're actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone else can put thoughts into your mind or take thoughts out of it?\n\nPatient: Put thoughts... put thoughts into my mind? Or take them out? No, no, no, that’s... that’s not how it works. That sounds like something out of a movie. My thoughts are entirely my own. I mean, if anything, the problem is that I have too many of my own thoughts, you know? \n\nLike I was saying before about the tracking and the route slips and making sure everything is perfectly aligned... my brain is just constantly running, constantly analyzing every single detail, every look, every shift in the schedule. Nobody is putting those thoughts there. I’m the one generating them because I’m the only one actually paying attention to what’s going on around the depot. \n\nAnd as for taking thoughts out? Honestly, I wish! I really, really wish someone could just reach in and scoop out the compliance regulations and the passenger counts and the traffic grids for just, I don't know, five minutes so I could actually relax when I sit on my couch at home. But no, they are all jammed in there, and they stay right there. It’s just me in my head. \n\nI mean, they can watch me, obviously. They can document what I do, they can whisper about me behind my back in the breakroom, but they can’t get inside my actual brain. They have to rely on external things, which is exactly why they do the nodding and the mirror checking that I told you about. If they could just beam thoughts into my head or steal my thoughts, they wouldn’t need to go through all that trouble with watching me, would they? They wouldn't need to be so sneaky about it in the dispatch office. So, no, my mind is entirely my own jurisdiction. It’s a very, very crowded jurisdiction right now, and it's exhausting to keep up with it all, but it's all me. Nobody else is in there.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs and experiences been going on continuously for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Well, continuously... yes, I suppose you could say that. It hasn't really stopped, not for a single day. If I think back on exactly when this all started to really ramp up... it's been about eight months now. Yes, eight months. \n\nIt was right around the time they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. You see, our regular lead, Tom, he had to take an unexpected medical leave—something with his back, or at least that's what they told us, though I have my own thoughts on the timing of that, but anyway. They needed someone to step up and cover the desk, and because I've always been so meticulous with my route reports and my scheduling, they picked me. And at first, I thought, okay, this is a great opportunity. I'm going to be sitting at the central desk, I'll have access to all the master grids, the real-time GPS tracking for the entire fleet, the direct lines to compliance... I thought it would be a good thing.\n\nBut that's when I really started to see it all. When you're just driving your own route, you only see your own little piece of the puzzle, right? You see your passengers, your traffic lights, your specific schedule. But when they put me in that lead role eight months ago, suddenly I was looking at the whole board. I was seeing how all the pieces were moving at once. And that's when I noticed the discrepancies. That's when I saw how the compliance officers would log certain delays but completely ignore others, and how Sarah in management started having these closed-door meetings that weren't on the official calendar. \n\nAnd even after Tom came back and I went back to my regular driving route, I couldn't unsee it. Once you realize how closely everything is being monitored, how the schedules are being manipulated behind the scenes, you can't just turn that awareness off. So yes, for the last eight months, it's been building and building. Every day I go in, I notice another detail, another look, another slight shift in the route maps. It's just been this constant, steady accumulation of evidence since I sat at that dispatch desk. So, definitely more than six months. Eight months of just... having my eyes wide open to it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers can control your actions or move your body from afar?\n\nPatient: Control my actions? Move my body? No, no, I... I'm the one driving the bus, Doctor. Literally and figuratively. I'm the one steering the wheel, I'm the one pressing the brake pedal, I'm the one opening and closing the doors at every single stop along Route 42. Nobody is, like, pulling my strings like a puppet. \n\nI mean, if they could control my actions, they wouldn't need to try so hard to intimidate me, would they? They wouldn't need to do the nodding, or the mirror checking, or the little subtle shifts in the breakroom schedules. If Sarah could just make me walk into her office and hand over my logbook, she would. But she can't. She has to wait for me to submit it at the end of my shift, exactly at 4:15 PM, like I always do. She has to rely on the system, on the rules, because she has absolutely no physical control over me. \n\nMy body is my own. When I get up in the morning, when I put on my uniform—the blue shirt, the navy trousers, the little name tag that never quite sits straight no matter how many times I adjust the pin—I'm doing all of that. My hands, my arms, my legs. It's all me. \n\nAnd honestly, if someone else were controlling my body, I think I'd be a lot less exhausted at the end of the day. Do you know how much physical tension it takes to drive a forty-foot vehicle through downtown while simultaneously monitoring every single passenger and keeping an eye out for whatever the dispatch office is planning next? My shoulders are practically up to my ears by the time I clock out. If someone else were moving my arms, maybe I wouldn't have this constant knot right here between my shoulder blades. But no, it's me. I'm the one doing the work. I'm the one doing all the moving. They just watch what I do. They can't make me do anything.\n\nDoctor: During the times you’ve felt most certain that dispatch and your coworkers were targeting you, have you also had a period of feeling unusually depressed or unusually energized with much less need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Or... unusually energized? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, don't get me wrong, the whole situation is incredibly stressful, like I was telling you about the knot in my shoulders and constantly having to watch the mirrors and monitor the dispatchers. But depressed? Like, staying in bed all day and crying or giving up? No, absolutely not. \n\nI have a job to do. I have Route 42 to run, and I take that responsibility very seriously. I am up every single morning at 4:30 AM sharp when my alarm goes off. I make my coffee—just black, because I don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when I need to be at the depot by 5:15 to do a proper, thorough pre-trip inspection of my vehicle before they even open the main gates. I check the tire pressure, the fluid levels, the air brakes... I do all of it. So I'm not, you know, moping around or losing interest in my life. I'm just highly focused. Highly, highly focused on the situation at hand and making sure I don't make a single mistake they can use against me. \n\nAnd as for being unusually energized or not needing sleep? Oh, believe me, Doctor, I need sleep. I am so incredibly tired by the time I get home. Like I said before, the physical and mental toll of driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic while simultaneously keeping track of all the surveillance and the coded route slips... it drains you. It completely drains you. I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying up all night scrubbing my kitchen baseboards or whatever people do when they have too much energy. \n\nBy the time 9:00 PM rolls around, I am practically collapsing into my bed. I *want* to sleep. Now, sometimes it takes a while to actually drift off because my brain is still reviewing the day's events, trying to piece together what Sarah meant when she deliberately moved the breakroom chair two inches to the left, but I definitely *need* the rest. I'm not running on two hours of sleep and feeling like a superhero. I'm just... I'm just a normal person trying to do her job safely while everyone at the dispatch office is playing these elaborate games, you know? So no, no extreme moods like that. Just the completely normal exhaustion of dealing with all of this day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the dispatcher or coworkers are inserting or broadcasting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Inserting thoughts? Like... beaming things into my brain? No, doctor, no. It’s nothing like a science fiction movie or anything like that. My thoughts are completely my own, just like my body is my own. I know exactly what I'm thinking. \n\nIf they could just put thoughts in my head or read my mind, they wouldn't have to go through all this elaborate trouble to monitor me in the real world, would they? They don't need telepathy because they have the technology right there at the depot. That's actually the whole reason I'm here, why this has been so incredibly stressful. I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. It’s the CAD system—Computer-Aided Dispatch. Every single time I have to detour, even if it's just one block because of that water main break on 4th Avenue last week, it gets flagged on their end. \n\nAnd then they make these little comments on the two-way radio. Just tiny, subtle things that probably sound completely harmless to anyone else listening in, but I know exactly what they mean. They'll say something like, \"Route 42, check your spacing,\" when I am exactly, perfectly on time, down to the second. \n\nAnd I know what people say about it. I’ve tried to bring it up, and people say, \"Oh, it’s just normal oversight, they track all the buses, it's just standard protocol for the transit authority.\" But it's not just normal oversight. I know the difference. I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me, too. Like Gary, who drives Route 15. Our routes overlap at the downtown transit center for exactly four minutes every morning at 11:15 AM, and I see him sitting in his cab, writing things down on his clipboard, looking right at my windshield. And then, almost like clockwork, two minutes later compliance is suddenly asking for my status update on the radio. \n\nSo, no, they aren't in my head. They don't need to be in my head. They’re tracking my every move through the computers, the radios, and the other drivers acting as informants. It's all happening right out in the open. You just have to be paying close enough attention to see how all the pieces connect.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers are doing anything impossible or supernatural, like using telepathy, reading your mind, or controlling events from a distance?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, Doctor, no, it’s really nothing like that. No magic, no supernatural powers, no telepathy or controlling things with their minds. Honestly, I feel like I’m repeating myself a bit here, but I really need you to grasp the reality of this, because it’s exactly why I’m sitting in this office right now. It’s not some impossible sci-fi movie plot. It’s very real, very grounded technology, which honestly makes it so much worse because there's nowhere to hide from it. \n\nI'm here because dispatch and compliance have been relentlessly tracking my route changes through the computer system. The CAD system is incredibly precise, you see. It updates the GPS coordinates every three seconds. And I know—believe me, I know—what everyone says about it. I’ve tried to explain this to my sister, I tried to bring it up to the union rep, and they all just wave me off and say, \"Oh, it’s just normal oversight, it’s a city transit authority, of course they track the buses for efficiency and safety.\" But I am out there on the road for eight, sometimes nine hours a day, and I can tell you with absolute certainty that it is *not* normal oversight. \n\nIf it were just normal oversight, they wouldn't be making those little comments on the radio. Just... just these tiny, pointed micro-aggressions broadcast over the open channel. Like, I’ll have to detour around a stalled delivery truck on 8th Street, a completely standard, by-the-book maneuver. I check my mirrors, I signal, I merge over—and the absolute second my left front tire crosses the dotted line, the radio crackles. \"Dispatch to all units, ensure you are strictly adhering to approved thoroughfares.\" It’s directed at me. It’s always directed at me. \n\nAnd it’s not just the people in the dispatch office sitting behind the computer screens, either. Wait, let me just explain this part because it's crucial to understanding the whole picture—I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me, too. I mentioned Gary earlier with his clipboard, but it’s Brenda, too. Brenda drives the 60-foot articulated bus on Route 9. Tuesday morning, she pulled into the layover zone at the exact same time as me, which isn't even on her scheduled timetable, and she parked at this very specific, deliberate angle so her side mirror was perfectly aligned with my cab. She sat there for ten minutes, just typing on her phone and looking up at me. Typing and looking. Typing and looking. \n\nSo, you see, they don't need to control events from a distance with magic or read my mind. They don't need anything supernatural. They have the computers, they have the radios, and they have each other acting as informants. It’s a completely physical, real-world surveillance operation they're running against me, right out in the open.\n\nDoctor: What would you say is the strongest evidence that dispatch and your coworkers are targeting you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor, I feel like I've been laying out the evidence this whole time. I mean, the strongest evidence is exactly what I've been trying to explain to you today. It’s the... it's the sheer, undeniable coordination of it all. \n\nI’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, and it's not just some vague feeling I have, it's literally documented in the CAD logs. Every single second, every slight deviation, it's all recorded. And then, right on the heels of that computer tracking, you get those little comments on the radio. It's the timing of it that proves it. \n\nIt’s just so incredibly frustrating because... well, because even when people say it’s just normal oversight, I know for a fact it isn't. People love to say that to me. My sister, the union reps, they just sigh and say, \"Oh, it's just normal oversight, it's standard procedure for the city transit authority, everyone gets tracked.\" They say it like I'm just completely misunderstanding my own profession. But I have been driving for years! I know exactly what normal oversight looks like. Normal oversight is asking for a passenger count or warning the fleet about a street sweeper on 5th Avenue. It is *not* a dispatcher making a hyper-specific, coded comment about adhering to approved thoroughfares the literal second my left tire crosses a lane line to safely bypass a stalled delivery truck. \n\nAnd the absolute clincher, the thing that proves it’s not just some glitch in the system or one overzealous dispatcher with a grudge, is the coworkers. I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. Like I was saying before about Gary and Brenda... you don't just sit at a downtown transit center with a clipboard staring directly at another driver's windshield for four minutes straight for no reason. You don't park your sixty-foot bus at a bizarre, totally unnecessary angle during a layover just to keep my cab perfectly framed in your side mirror while you furiously type on your phone. They are acting as eyes on the ground for dispatch. \n\nSo when you take the computer tracking, the radio comments, and the other drivers acting as informants all working perfectly in sync... I mean, what more evidence do you need? It’s a complete, closed loop of surveillance. That's the strongest evidence. It's the fact that all these different pieces—the technology, the communications, the people—are all perfectly aligned to monitor my every move, no matter how much everyone else tries to brush it off as just doing their jobs.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I... I really don't understand why we keep coming back to this idea of mind control or impossible technology. I feel like I’m not being heard, or maybe you're trying to fit me into some specific box, but I've been completely clear about this from the start. There are no hidden means. It's the everyday tools of the job being weaponized against me. 

Like I keep saying, I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. The regular, standard-issue CAD system that every transit vehicle in the city is equipped with. It uses standard GPS, standard cellular data networks. There is absolutely nothing impossible about it. And the little comments on the radio? Those come through the exact same standard Motorola two-way radios we've been using for the last six years. They just use them to send these coded, perfectly timed messages to me, right out in the open for anyone to hear if they actually knew what to listen for. 

And I know everyone wants to dismiss it. Believe me, I've heard it all. But even when people say it’s just normal oversight, when they try to tell me I'm just stressed or misinterpreting standard transit protocol... they aren't out there in the driver's seat. They don't see what I see day in and day out. Because beyond the computers and the radios, I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. Real, physical people. My co-workers. 

They don't need some futuristic, impossible technology when they have an entire network of drivers just writing things down on clipboards and texting dispatch from their layover spots. It’s a very real, very physical, very possible system of surveillance. I just really need someone to actually look at the facts I'm giving them instead of asking me about mind control again, because it's all happening right there in the real world.

They are acting as eyes on the ground for dispatch. \n\nSo when you take the computer tracking, the radio comments, and the other drivers acting as informants all working perfectly in sync... I mean, what more evidence do you need? It’s a complete, closed loop of surveillance. That's the strongest evidence. It's the fact that all these different pieces—the technology, the communications, the people—are all perfectly aligned to monitor my every move, no matter how much everyone else tries to brush it off as just doing their jobs.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the people at dispatch are using any hidden or impossible means, like mind control or technology far beyond normal surveillance, to target you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... There are no secret microchips or lasers or anything you'd see in a comic book. It’s the exact opposite, which is what makes it so terrifying. It's the everyday tools of the job being weaponized against me. \n\nLike I keep saying, I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. The regular, standard-issue CAD system that every transit vehicle in the city is equipped with. It uses standard GPS, standard cellular data networks. There is absolutely nothing impossible about it. And the little comments on the radio? Those come through the exact same standard Motorola two-way radios we've been using for the last six years. They just use them to send these coded, perfectly timed messages to me, right out in the open for anyone to hear if they actually knew what to listen for. \n\nAnd I know everyone wants to dismiss it. My co-workers. \n\nThey don't need some futuristic, impossible technology when they have an entire network of drivers just writing things down on clipboards and texting dispatch from their layover spots. Doctor, honestly... no. I don't think it's possible. I really, truly don't. And I know that's what everyone wants to hear from me. They want me to just throw my hands up, sigh, and say, "Oh, silly me, I guess it's all just a big misunderstanding, I guess I'm just stressed out." But if you were in my shoes, sitting behind that massive steering wheel for nine hours a day, watching it all unfold in real-time, you wouldn't think it was a coincidence either. 

I mean, I feel like a broken record, but I'm here, talking to you, specifically because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. It's relentless, and it’s the combination of that pinpoint computer tracking and those little comments on the radio that makes it completely impossible to ignore. You just can't chalk that up to chance or a misunderstanding. If it happened once, sure. Twice, maybe. But every single shift? 

And even when people say it’s just normal oversight—which, like I keep saying, is everyone's favorite excuse to throw at me—it completely fails to explain the human element. It doesn't explain why I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. You think Brenda just happens to park her sixty-foot bus at that exact, bizarre angle every Tuesday just for fun? You think Gary just happens to be scribbling on his clipboard staring at my windshield every single time I pull into the downtown transit center? 

It’s not just one isolated thing, Doctor. If it was *just* the radio, maybe I could say I misheard. If it was *just* the CAD system, sure, I could buy the "normal oversight" line. But it’s all of it together, working perfectly in sync. The computers, the radios, the coworkers. So, no. I don't think it's possible that I'm wrong about this. I know exactly what they are doing, and I just need someone to actually believe me instead of trying to talk me out of my own reality.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... well, it started a few weeks ago, maybe a month, down at the depot. I drive transit, you know, the city buses, and dispatch has been—well, they've been doing things. Tracking me. And not just the normal GPS tracking, because obviously they have to know where the buses are, I get that, that's just part of the job. But it's the route changes. They keep making these tiny little adjustments to my schedule, and I know they're monitoring how I react to it through the computer system in the cab. And then there's the radio. The dispatcher will make these little comments, just these offhand remarks that sound normal to everyone else, but I know they're directed at me. Like, 'watch your speed on 4th,' when I'm not even on 4th yet, but they know I'm about to turn there. And my supervisor, she tells me, 'Oh, it's just normal oversight, everyone gets audited, it's just standard compliance,' but I'm not stupid. I can tell. I see the way the other drivers look at me in the breakroom. Certain drivers, especially the ones on the early shift, I know they're reporting back on me. They're watching me punch in, watching how long I take to do my pre-trip inspection, and they're feeding it all back to compliance. So, yeah, that's... that's why I'm here. Because it's just getting to be a lot to deal with every single shift.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? Like... like imaginary voices? No, no, definitely not. It's nothing like that. I mean, my hearing is perfectly fine, you know? Actually, it's probably better than fine, because when you're sitting in that driver's seat for eight, sometimes nine hours a day if I pick up an extra shift, you really have to be hyper-aware of every single sound around you. You're listening to the engine hum, the pneumatic doors hissing, the air brakes, the passengers shuffling around in the back... and of course, you're always, always listening to the radio. \n\nBut it's not like I'm sitting there hearing ghosts or, you know, invisible people talking to me in my head. The things I hear are completely real. When dispatch comes over the comms, that is a real person, sitting down at the control center, speaking into a real microphone. It's just... it's what they choose to say, and how they say it, that's the problem. Like I mentioned with the 4th street thing earlier? That was a real voice over the speaker, it was Greg from the afternoon shift, but he was saying things he shouldn't have known to say at that exact moment. And in the breakroom, when the other drivers are huddled up by the coffee maker and talking low, I might not always make out every single word they're whispering, but I hear the tone. I hear the way they suddenly stop talking when I walk over to the vending machine. So, no, I'm not hearing things that aren't there. I'm just hearing exactly what they're doing, and they think I don't notice it, but I absolutely do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your thinking or behavior that other people have said seem disorganized or unusual?\n\nPatient: Well, 'unusual' is such a subjective word, isn't it? I mean, who gets to decide what's usual? But... I suppose, yes, my supervisor and a couple of the other drivers have made some comments lately about how I'm doing things. Like, in the dispatch office before my shift, there's this big magnetic board where we have all the route slips and driver assignments. And recently, I've just had to make sure they're arranged... properly. Not just thrown up there haphazardly, because that's how mistakes happen, that's how the whole system gets thrown off. So I'll spend some time—maybe a little longer than I used to, I admit—moving the magnets around, lining them up in a very specific pattern, making sure the colors coordinate with the shift times and the route numbers. It just has to be right, or else the whole board feels like it's going wrong, like the whole day is going to derail. \n\nAnd then... well, there's this other thing. When people talk to me, especially the other drivers or dispatch, I've found myself needing to repeat the last few words they say before I answer them. Like, if Greg says, 'Bus 42 is ready for you,' I'll say, 'ready for you... yeah, I'll take the keys.' It just sort of slips out. I don't even always realize I'm doing it until I see their faces. They get this look, like I'm being difficult on purpose or trying to mock them, but I'm not! I'm really not. I'm just... I have to process it that way to make sure I've got it exactly right, because if I miss a detail, if I don't repeat it to lock it in, who knows what they'll report back to compliance? But they don't understand that. They just stare at me and whisper to each other when they think I'm out of earshot, calling me weird or saying I'm disorganized, when really I'm the only one trying to keep everything perfectly organized so the whole system doesn't collapse.\n\nDoctor: How much have these experiences affected your ability to do your job?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely having an impact, I won't deny that. I mean, I try to keep everything together, I really do, but with all the tracking and the magnets and making sure I don't miss a single word anyone says... it takes up so much time. And energy. So, yeah, at work, things have been slipping. I'm usually so good about my route reports, you know, filling out every little detail about passenger counts and traffic delays and any mechanical issues, but lately I've just... I've fallen so far behind on them. When I have to relay instructions over the radio, or talk to the other drivers during shift changes, I guess... I guess I've gotten confusing. At least, that's what they say. The other drivers have been complaining to my supervisor, saying they can't follow what I'm telling them, which is incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to be precise! But they get annoyed, and then they report me, and it just makes the whole situation worse. \n\nAnd honestly, it doesn't stop when I clock out. It bleeds into everything. When I get home, I'm just so drained from being on high alert all day, from analyzing every word and every look, that I just... I can't do anything. My husband, bless him, he's had to take over basically everything. He's doing all the cooking now, making all the meals, which he never used to have to do because I always had a meal plan for the week, color-coded and everything on the fridge. But now he's doing that, and he's handling all the phone calls, paying the bills, doing all the basic planning for the house... because I just can't keep up. I sit down on the couch and my mind is just racing with everything from the depot, and I can't even figure out what we're supposed to have for dinner, let alone actually make it. So, yes, it's... it's affecting a lot. It's affecting everything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no, I don't see things. You have to see everything—every pedestrian stepping off the curb, every cyclist weaving between lanes, the exact timing of the yellow lights. When I have to relay instructions over the radio, or talk to the other drivers during shift changes, I guess... I guess I've gotten confusing. At least, that's what they say. The other drivers have been complaining to my supervisor, saying they can't follow what I'm telling them, which is incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to be precise! But they get annoyed, and then they report me, and it just makes the whole situation worse. \n\nAnd honestly, it doesn't stop when I clock out. It bleeds into everything. When I get home, I'm just so drained from being on high alert all day, from analyzing every word and every look, that I just... I can't do anything. My husband, bless him, he's had to take over basically everything. He's doing all the cooking now, making all the meals, which he never used to have to do because I always had a meal plan for the week, color-coded and everything on the fridge. But now he's doing that, and he's handling all the phone calls, paying the bills, doing all the basic planning for the house... because I just can't keep up. I sit down on the couch and my mind is just racing with everything from the depot, and I can't even figure out what we're supposed to have for dinner, let alone actually make it. So, yes, it's... it's affecting a lot. It's affecting everything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no, I don't see things. I mean, my vision is 20/20, which you absolutely need when you're driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic during rush hour. You have to see everything—every pedestrian stepping off the curb, every cyclist weaving between lanes, the exact timing of the yellow lights. But I'm not seeing... like, hallucinations or whatever you want to call it. I'm not seeing monsters or angels or things that aren't physically standing right there on the pavement. \n\nEverything I see is completely, one hundred percent real. It's just... I notice the details that other people miss, or maybe they just pretend they don't see them. Like the way the dispatch manager, Sarah, will look at Greg right after I walk into the room, and they do this little nod. It's a real nod, I see it happen with my own two eyes, but it's what the nod means that's the issue. Or the way a passenger will get on the bus, scan their pass, and then look directly into the rearview mirror at me for exactly three seconds before sitting down. They are really doing that. I see them do it. I'm not making it up, I'm not hallucinating the passenger or the mirror or the look. \n\nSo, no, I don't see things that aren't there. I just see exactly what is there, and I see how it all connects, even when everyone else is walking around with blinders on, acting like everything is completely normal when it's obviously not. I see the way the route maps in the breakroom have been slightly shifted, just a fraction of an inch, but enough to change the grid alignment. That's a real physical change, not a vision. So, no, my eyes are perfectly fine. I just see what they're actually doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone else can put thoughts into your mind or take thoughts out of it?\n\nPatient: Put thoughts... put thoughts into my mind? Or take them out? No, no, no, that’s... that’s not how it works. That sounds like something out of a movie. My thoughts are entirely my own. I mean, if anything, the problem is that I have too many of my own thoughts, you know? \n\nLike I was saying before about the tracking and the route slips and making sure everything is perfectly aligned... my brain is just constantly running, constantly analyzing every single detail, every look, every shift in the schedule. Nobody is putting those thoughts there. I’m the one generating them because I’m the only one actually paying attention to what’s going on around the depot. \n\nAnd as for taking thoughts out? Honestly, I wish! I really, really wish someone could just reach in and scoop out the compliance regulations and the passenger counts and the traffic grids for just, I don't know, five minutes so I could actually relax when I sit on my couch at home. But no, they are all jammed in there, and they stay right there. It’s just me in my head. \n\nI mean, they can watch me, obviously. They can document what I do, they can whisper about me behind my back in the breakroom, but they can’t get inside my actual brain. They have to rely on external things, which is exactly why they do the nodding and the mirror checking that I told you about. If they could just beam thoughts into my head or steal my thoughts, they wouldn’t need to go through all that trouble with watching me, would they? They wouldn't need to be so sneaky about it in the dispatch office. So, no, my mind is entirely my own jurisdiction. It’s a very, very crowded jurisdiction right now, and it's exhausting to keep up with it all, but it's all me. Nobody else is in there.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs and experiences been going on continuously for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Well, continuously... yes, I suppose you could say that. It hasn't really stopped, not for a single day. If I think back on exactly when this all started to really ramp up... it's been about eight months now. Yes, eight months. \n\nIt was right around the time they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. You see, our regular lead, Tom, he had to take an unexpected medical leave—something with his back, or at least that's what they told us, though I have my own thoughts on the timing of that, but anyway. They needed someone to step up and cover the desk, and because I've always been so meticulous with my route reports and my scheduling, they picked me. And at first, I thought, okay, this is a great opportunity. I'm going to be sitting at the central desk, I'll have access to all the master grids, the real-time GPS tracking for the entire fleet, the direct lines to compliance... I thought it would be a good thing.\n\nBut that's when I really started to see it all. When you're just driving your own route, you only see your own little piece of the puzzle, right? You see your passengers, your traffic lights, your specific schedule. But when they put me in that lead role eight months ago, suddenly I was looking at the whole board. I was seeing how all the pieces were moving at once. And that's when I noticed the discrepancies. That's when I saw how the compliance officers would log certain delays but completely ignore others, and how Sarah in management started having these closed-door meetings that weren't on the official calendar. \n\nAnd even after Tom came back and I went back to my regular driving route, I couldn't unsee it. Once you realize how closely everything is being monitored, how the schedules are being manipulated behind the scenes, you can't just turn that awareness off. So yes, for the last eight months, it's been building and building. Every day I go in, I notice another detail, another look, another slight shift in the route maps. It's just been this constant, steady accumulation of evidence since I sat at that dispatch desk. So, definitely more than six months. Eight months of just... having my eyes wide open to it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers can control your actions or move your body from afar?\n\nPatient: Control my actions? Move my body? No, no, I... I'm the one driving the bus, Doctor. Literally and figuratively. I'm the one steering the wheel, I'm the one pressing the brake pedal, I'm the one opening and closing the doors at every single stop along Route 42. Nobody is, like, pulling my strings like a puppet. \n\nI mean, if they could control my actions, they wouldn't need to try so hard to intimidate me, would they? They wouldn't need to do the nodding, or the mirror checking, or the little subtle shifts in the breakroom schedules. If Sarah could just make me walk into her office and hand over my logbook, she would. But she can't. She has to wait for me to submit it at the end of my shift, exactly at 4:15 PM, like I always do. She has to rely on the system, on the rules, because she has absolutely no physical control over me. \n\nMy body is my own. When I get up in the morning, when I put on my uniform—the blue shirt, the navy trousers, the little name tag that never quite sits straight no matter how many times I adjust the pin—I'm doing all of that. My hands, my arms, my legs. It's all me. \n\nAnd honestly, if someone else were controlling my body, I think I'd be a lot less exhausted at the end of the day. Do you know how much physical tension it takes to drive a forty-foot vehicle through downtown while simultaneously monitoring every single passenger and keeping an eye out for whatever the dispatch office is planning next? My shoulders are practically up to my ears by the time I clock out. If someone else were moving my arms, maybe I wouldn't have this constant knot right here between my shoulder blades. But no, it's me. I'm the one doing the work. I'm the one doing all the moving. They just watch what I do. They can't make me do anything.\n\nDoctor: During the times you’ve felt most certain that dispatch and your coworkers were targeting you, have you also had a period of feeling unusually depressed or unusually energized with much less need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Or... unusually energized? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, don't get me wrong, the whole situation is incredibly stressful, like I was telling you about the knot in my shoulders and constantly having to watch the mirrors and monitor the dispatchers. But depressed? Like, staying in bed all day and crying or giving up? No, absolutely not. \n\nI have a job to do. I have Route 42 to run, and I take that responsibility very seriously. I am up every single morning at 4:30 AM sharp when my alarm goes off. I make my coffee—just black, because I don't have time to mess around with cream and sugar when I need to be at the depot by 5:15 to do a proper, thorough pre-trip inspection of my vehicle before they even open the main gates. I check the tire pressure, the fluid levels, the air brakes... I do all of it. So I'm not, you know, moping around or losing interest in my life. I'm just highly focused. Highly, highly focused on the situation at hand and making sure I don't make a single mistake they can use against me. \n\nAnd as for being unusually energized or not needing sleep? Oh, believe me, Doctor, I need sleep. I am so incredibly tired by the time I get home. Like I said before, the physical and mental toll of driving a forty-foot bus through downtown traffic while simultaneously keeping track of all the surveillance and the coded route slips... it drains you. It completely drains you. I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying up all night scrubbing my kitchen baseboards or whatever people do when they have too much energy. \n\nBy the time 9:00 PM rolls around, I am practically collapsing into my bed. I *want* to sleep. Now, sometimes it takes a while to actually drift off because my brain is still reviewing the day's events, trying to piece together what Sarah meant when she deliberately moved the breakroom chair two inches to the left, but I definitely *need* the rest. I'm not running on two hours of sleep and feeling like a superhero. I'm just... I'm just a normal person trying to do her job safely while everyone at the dispatch office is playing these elaborate games, you know? So no, no extreme moods like that. Just the completely normal exhaustion of dealing with all of this day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the dispatcher or coworkers are inserting or broadcasting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Inserting thoughts? Like... beaming things into my brain? No, doctor, no. It’s nothing like a science fiction movie or anything like that. My thoughts are completely my own, just like my body is my own. I know exactly what I'm thinking. \n\nIf they could just put thoughts in my head or read my mind, they wouldn't have to go through all this elaborate trouble to monitor me in the real world, would they? They don't need telepathy because they have the technology right there at the depot. That's actually the whole reason I'm here, why this has been so incredibly stressful. I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. It’s the CAD system—Computer-Aided Dispatch. Every single time I have to detour, even if it's just one block because of that water main break on 4th Avenue last week, it gets flagged on their end. \n\nAnd then they make these little comments on the two-way radio. Just tiny, subtle things that probably sound completely harmless to anyone else listening in, but I know exactly what they mean. They'll say something like, \"Route 42, check your spacing,\" when I am exactly, perfectly on time, down to the second. \n\nAnd I know what people say about it. I’ve tried to bring it up, and people say, \"Oh, it’s just normal oversight, they track all the buses, it's just standard protocol for the transit authority.\" But it's not just normal oversight. I know the difference. I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me, too. Like Gary, who drives Route 15. Our routes overlap at the downtown transit center for exactly four minutes every morning at 11:15 AM, and I see him sitting in his cab, writing things down on his clipboard, looking right at my windshield. And then, almost like clockwork, two minutes later compliance is suddenly asking for my status update on the radio. \n\nSo, no, they aren't in my head. They don't need to be in my head. They’re tracking my every move through the computers, the radios, and the other drivers acting as informants. It's all happening right out in the open. You just have to be paying close enough attention to see how all the pieces connect.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the dispatcher or coworkers are doing anything impossible or supernatural, like using telepathy, reading your mind, or controlling events from a distance?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, Doctor, no, it’s really nothing like that. No magic, no supernatural powers, no telepathy or controlling things with their minds. Honestly, I feel like I’m repeating myself a bit here, but I really need you to grasp the reality of this, because it’s exactly why I’m sitting in this office right now. It’s not some impossible sci-fi movie plot. It’s very real, very grounded technology, which honestly makes it so much worse because there's nowhere to hide from it. \n\nI'm here because dispatch and compliance have been relentlessly tracking my route changes through the computer system. The CAD system is incredibly precise, you see. It updates the GPS coordinates every three seconds. And I know—believe me, I know—what everyone says about it. I’ve tried to explain this to my sister, I tried to bring it up to the union rep, and they all just wave me off and say, \"Oh, it’s just normal oversight, it’s a city transit authority, of course they track the buses for efficiency and safety.\" But I am out there on the road for eight, sometimes nine hours a day, and I can tell you with absolute certainty that it is *not* normal oversight. \n\nIf it were just normal oversight, they wouldn't be making those little comments on the radio. Just... just these tiny, pointed micro-aggressions broadcast over the open channel. Like, I’ll have to detour around a stalled delivery truck on 8th Street, a completely standard, by-the-book maneuver. I check my mirrors, I signal, I merge over—and the absolute second my left front tire crosses the dotted line, the radio crackles. \"Dispatch to all units, ensure you are strictly adhering to approved thoroughfares.\" It’s directed at me. It’s always directed at me. \n\nAnd it’s not just the people in the dispatch office sitting behind the computer screens, either. Wait, let me just explain this part because it's crucial to understanding the whole picture—I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me, too. I mentioned Gary earlier with his clipboard, but it’s Brenda, too. Brenda drives the 60-foot articulated bus on Route 9. Tuesday morning, she pulled into the layover zone at the exact same time as me, which isn't even on her scheduled timetable, and she parked at this very specific, deliberate angle so her side mirror was perfectly aligned with my cab. She sat there for ten minutes, just typing on her phone and looking up at me. Typing and looking. Typing and looking. \n\nSo, you see, they don't need to control events from a distance with magic or read my mind. They don't need anything supernatural. They have the computers, they have the radios, and they have each other acting as informants. It’s a completely physical, real-world surveillance operation they're running against me, right out in the open.\n\nDoctor: What would you say is the strongest evidence that dispatch and your coworkers are targeting you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor, I feel like I've been laying out the evidence this whole time. I mean, the strongest evidence is exactly what I've been trying to explain to you today. It’s the... it's the sheer, undeniable coordination of it all. \n\nI’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, and it's not just some vague feeling I have, it's literally documented in the CAD logs. Every single second, every slight deviation, it's all recorded. And then, right on the heels of that computer tracking, you get those little comments on the radio. It's the timing of it that proves it. \n\nIt’s just so incredibly frustrating because... well, because even when people say it’s just normal oversight, I know for a fact it isn't. People love to say that to me. My sister, the union reps, they just sigh and say, \"Oh, it's just normal oversight, it's standard procedure for the city transit authority, everyone gets tracked.\" They say it like I'm just completely misunderstanding my own profession. But I have been driving for years! I know exactly what normal oversight looks like. Normal oversight is asking for a passenger count or warning the fleet about a street sweeper on 5th Avenue. It is *not* a dispatcher making a hyper-specific, coded comment about adhering to approved thoroughfares the literal second my left tire crosses a lane line to safely bypass a stalled delivery truck. \n\nAnd the absolute clincher, the thing that proves it’s not just some glitch in the system or one overzealous dispatcher with a grudge, is the coworkers. I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. Like I was saying before about Gary and Brenda... you don't just sit at a downtown transit center with a clipboard staring directly at another driver's windshield for four minutes straight for no reason. You don't park your sixty-foot bus at a bizarre, totally unnecessary angle during a layover just to keep my cab perfectly framed in your side mirror while you furiously type on your phone. They are acting as eyes on the ground for dispatch. \n\nSo when you take the computer tracking, the radio comments, and the other drivers acting as informants all working perfectly in sync... I mean, what more evidence do you need? It’s a complete, closed loop of surveillance. That's the strongest evidence. It's the fact that all these different pieces—the technology, the communications, the people—are all perfectly aligned to monitor my every move, no matter how much everyone else tries to brush it off as just doing their jobs.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the people at dispatch are using any hidden or impossible means, like mind control or technology far beyond normal surveillance, to target you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I really don't understand why we keep coming back to this idea of mind control or impossible technology. I feel like I’m not being heard, or maybe you're trying to fit me into some specific box, but I've been completely clear about this from the start. There are no hidden means. There are no secret microchips or lasers or anything you'd see in a comic book. It’s the exact opposite, which is what makes it so terrifying. It's the everyday tools of the job being weaponized against me. \n\nLike I keep saying, I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. The regular, standard-issue CAD system that every transit vehicle in the city is equipped with. It uses standard GPS, standard cellular data networks. There is absolutely nothing impossible about it. And the little comments on the radio? Those come through the exact same standard Motorola two-way radios we've been using for the last six years. They just use them to send these coded, perfectly timed messages to me, right out in the open for anyone to hear if they actually knew what to listen for. \n\nAnd I know everyone wants to dismiss it. Believe me, I've heard it all. But even when people say it’s just normal oversight, when they try to tell me I'm just stressed or misinterpreting standard transit protocol... they aren't out there in the driver's seat. They don't see what I see day in and day out. Because beyond the computers and the radios, I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. Real, physical people. My co-workers. \n\nThey don't need some futuristic, impossible technology when they have an entire network of drivers just writing things down on clipboards and texting dispatch from their layover spots. It’s a very real, very physical, very possible system of surveillance. I just really need someone to actually look at the facts I'm giving them instead of asking me about mind control again, because it's all happening right there in the real world.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your coworkers and dispatch are not actually targeting you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, honestly... no. I don't think it's possible. I really, truly don't. And I know that's what everyone wants to hear from me. They want me to just throw my hands up, sigh, and say, \"Oh, silly me, I guess it's all just a big misunderstanding, I guess I'm just stressed out.\" But if you were in my shoes, sitting behind that massive steering wheel for nine hours a day, watching it all unfold in real-time, you wouldn't think it was a coincidence either. \n\nI mean, I feel like a broken record, but I'm here, talking to you, specifically because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system. It's relentless, and it’s the combination of that pinpoint computer tracking and those little comments on the radio that makes it completely impossible to ignore. You just can't chalk that up to chance or a misunderstanding. If it happened once, sure. Twice, maybe. But every single shift? \n\nAnd even when people say it’s just normal oversight—which, like I keep saying, is everyone's favorite excuse to throw at me—it completely fails to explain the human element. It doesn't explain why I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. You think Brenda just happens to park her sixty-foot bus at that exact, bizarre angle every Tuesday just for fun? You think Gary just happens to be scribbling on his clipboard staring at my windshield every single time I pull into the downtown transit center? \n\nIt’s not just one isolated thing, Doctor. If it was *just* the radio, maybe I could say I misheard. If it was *just* the CAD system, sure, I could buy the \"normal oversight\" line. But it’s all of it together, working perfectly in sync. The computers, the radios, the coworkers. So, no. I don't think it's possible that I'm wrong about this. I know exactly what they are doing, and I just need someone to actually believe me instead of trying to talk me out of my own reality.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
